points,  and  reached  the  place  at  which  the  Ghit  road, 
leading  to  Egypt,  and  the  Tawdt  road  divide.  Here 
there  are  subterranean  water-beds  which  flow  over 
iron;  if  a  piece  of  white  cloth  is  washed  in  this  water 
it  turns  black. 

Ten  days  after  leaving  this  point  we  came  to  the 
country  of  Haggar,  who  are  a  tribe  of  Berbers;  they 
wear  race  veils  and  are  a  rascally  lot.'**  We  en¬ 
countered  one  of  their  chiefs,  who  held  up  the  caravan 
until  they  paid  him  an  indemnity  of  pieces  of  cloth 
and  other  goods.  Our  arrival  in  their  country  fell 
in  the  month  of  Ramaddn,  during  which  they  make 
no  raiding  expeditions  and  do  not  mole^  caravans. 
Even  their  robbers,  if  they  find  goods  on  the  road 
during  Ramadan,  do  not  touch  them.  This  is  the 
custom  of  all  the  Berbers  along  this  route.  We 
continued  to  travel  through  the  country  of  Haggar 
for  a  month;  it  has  few  plants,  is  very  ftony,  and  the 
road  through  it  is  bad.  On  the  feftival  of  the  Faft- 
breaking  we  reached  the  country  of  some  Berbers, 
who  wear  the  face-veils,  like  these  others. 

We  came  next  to  Bddd,  one  of  the  principal  villages 
of  Tawdt.  The  soil  there  is  all  sand  and  saltmarsh; 
there  are  quantities  of  dates,  but  they  are  not  good, 
though  the  local  inhabitants  prefer  them  to  the  dates 
of  Sijilmdsa.  There  are  no  crops  there,  nor  butter, 
nor  olive  oil ;  all  these  things  have  to  be  imported  from 
the  Maghrib.  The  food  of  its  inhabitants  consifts  of 
dates  and  locufts,  for  thefe  are  quantities  of  locudls 
in  their  country;  they  dlore  them  juft  like  dates  and 
use  them  as  food.  They  go  out  to  catch  the  locufts 
before  sunrise,  for  at  that  hour  they  cannot  fly  on 
account  of  the  cold.'*^ 

We  ftayed  at  Bddd  for  some  days,  and  then  joined 
a  caravan  and  in  the  middle  of  Dhu’l-qa'da  reached

TRAVELS  OF  IBN  BATTUTA 

the  city  of  Sijilmdsa.  I  set  out  thence  on  the  second 
of  Dhu’l-hijja  [29th  December],  at  a  time  of  intense 
cold,  and  snow  fell  very  heavily  on  the  way.  I  have 
in  my  life  seen  bad  roads  and  quantities  of  snow,  at 
Bukhdrd  and  Samarqand,  in  Khurdsdn,  and  the  lands 
of  the  Turks,  but  never  have  I  seen  anything  worse 
than  the  road  of  Umm  Junayba.  On  the  eve  of  the 
Festival  we  reached  Ddr  at-Tama‘.  I  stayed  there 
during  the  day  of  the  fea^  and  then  went  on.  So 
I  arrived  at  the  royal  city  of  Fa’s  [Fez],  the  capital 
of  our  mafter  the  Commander  of  the  Faithful  (may 
God  ftrengthen  him),  where  I  kissed  his  beneficent 
hand  and  was  privileged  to  behold  his  gracious  coun¬ 
tenance.  [Here]  I  settled  down  under  the  wing  of 
his  bounty  after  long  journeying.  May  God  Moft 
High  recompense  him  for  the  abundant  favours  and 
ample  benefits  which  he  has  bellowed  on  me;  may  He 
prolong  his  days  and  spare  him  to  the  Muslims  for 
many  years  to  come. 

Here  ends  the  travel-narrative  entitled  A  Donation 
to  those  interested  in  the  Curiosities  of  the  Cities  and 
Marvels  of  the  Ways.  Its  diftation  was  finished  on 
3rd  Dhu’l-hijja  756  [9th  December  1355].  Praise 
be  to  God,  and  peace  to  His  creatures  whom  He  hath 
chosen. 

Ibn  Juzayy  adds:  “Here  ends  the  narrative  which 
I  have  abridged  from  the  diftation  of  the  Shaykh  Abd 
‘Abdalldh  Muhammad  ibn  Battdta  (may  God  ennoble 
him).  It  is  plain  to  any  man  of  intelligence  that 
this  shaykh  is  the  traveller  of  the  age :  and  if  one  were 
to  say  “  the  traveller  par  excellence  of  this  our  Muslim 
community  ”  he  would  be  guilty  of  no  exaggeration.

This  is  a  popular  tradition,  intended  to  account  for  the  fad  that 
the  Church  of  St.  John  was  not  converted  into  a  mosque  until  seventy 
years  after  the  Arab  conqued  of  Damascus.  The  church  was  not 
demolished,  but  merely  dripped  of  its  Chridian  furnishings  and  refitted 
as  a  mosque.  Ibn  Battfita  goes  on  to  give  a  detailed  description  of  the 
mosque  as  it  exided  in  his  day.  Tins  edifice  was  dedroyed  by  fire 
during  Tamerlane’s  occupation  of  Damascus  in  1400,  and  has  since 
been  recondruded  more  than  once.  The  present  building  dates  only 
from  1893,  and  preserves  little  trace  of  its  former  magnificence,  except 
the  three  line  minarets.

coppersmiths  frill  occupies  the  same  position,  but  is  by  no  means  “  one 
of  the  finefr  in  Damascus  ’*  at  the  present  day. 

This  was  originally  a  mechanical  water-clock,  which  was  frill 
in  working  order  when  Ibn  Jubayr  visited  Damascus  in  1184  (see 
le  Strange,  Palefiine  under  the  Moslems y  p.  250),  but  had  fallen 
out  of  repair  in  the  interval.  Though  the  galleries  have  long  since 
disappeared,  the  spouting  fountain  (a  relic  of  Byzantine  days)  still 
exists.

The  word  qaysariya  is  defined  variously  as  “  a  public  place  in 
which  the  market  is  held  ”  or  “  a  square  building  containing  chambers, 
^orerooms,  and  ^alls  for  merchants.”  The  name  is  evidently  derived 
from  the  Latin  or  Greek,  and  was  used  orginally  only  by  the  Arabs  of 
Syria  and  North  Africa,  but  its  origin  is  obscure.  Of  various  theories 
wluch  have  been  advanced  the  mo^  probable  is  that  it  means  a  market 
building,  privileged  or  authorized  by  the  ruler  (originally  in  these 
countries,  of  course,  the  Csesar)  in  return  for  a  certain  fixed  payment, 
but  no  corresponding  term  has  been  found  in  the  Byzantine  hi^ories. 
It  is  now  apphed  to  the  principal  market  of  a  town,  and  I  have  heard 
the  term  us^  in  North  Africa  (Tlemsen)  for  the  main  shopping  ^eet. 
The  provision  of  gates  for  bazaars  was,  and  ^Hll  is,  quite  common. 
(Dozy  s.v. ;  le  Str^ge,  La/f^s  of  the  Eastern  Caliphate ^  p.  89). 

Sinjir  is  evidently  misplaced.  It  is  mo^t  probable  that  it  was

as  the  frontier  fortress  over  again^  the  Persian  territories.

The  underground  waterbearing  beds  of  eastern  Arabia  discharge 
into  the  sea  at  Bahrayn.  During  the  Turkish  occupation,  the  sailors 
used  to  dive  into  the  sea  and  bring  up  fresh  water  in  a  leather  sack  for 
the  use  of  the  commander,  and  the  Portuguese  supplied  themselves  in 
the  same  way  by  pumps.  There  is  a  dlory  that  a  camel  once  fell  into 
a  spring  at  al-Hasd,  and  was  never  seen  again  until  it  came  up  in  the 
sea  near  Bahrayn.

^  Bildd  ar-Mm^  literally  “  the  land  of  the  Greeks,”  though  used 
of  the  Byzantine  territories  generally,  was  naturally  applied  more 
specially  to  the  frontier  province  of  Anatolia.  After  some  temporary 
conquests  in  earlier  centuries,  it  had  been  finally  overrun  by  the  Saljfiq 
Turb  between  1071  and  1081.  Down  to  the  end  of  the  thirteenth 
century,  the  whole  peninsula,  except  those  seAions  which  were  held 
by  the  Chri^ans  (Byzantium,  Trebizond,  and  Armenia)  or  the  ruler 
of  ^Irdq,  owed  allegiance  to  the  Saljfiq  sultan  of  Konia,  but  from  a 
little  before  1 300  it  was  parcelled  out  between  a  score  of  local  chiefs, 
whose  territories  were  gradually  absorbed  into  the  Ottoman  Empire.

ously  deficient  in  wood,  has  always  needed  to  import  large  quantities 
of  it  for  the  building  of  fleets,  etc.

engaged  in  combating  the  fanatical  Shi‘ite  se6b  in  Syria.  The 
members  of  this  warrior  guild,  whose  rule  it  was  that  no  member 
should  call  for  assignee  in  any  misfortune  that  might  befall  him, 
elected  suitable  persons  and  similarly  invefted  them  with  trousers  on 
their  admission. 

In  1182  the  Caliph  an-Ndsir,  having  been  inve^ed  with  the  libds 
or  trousers  by  a  Siiff  shaykh,  conceived  the  idea  of  organizing  the 
Futuwa  on  the  lines  of  an  order  of  Chivalry  (probably  on  the  Frankish 
model),  con^tuted  himself  sovereign  of  the  order,  and  bellowed  the 
libh  as  its  insignia  on  the  ruling  princes  and  other  personages  of  his 
time.  The  ceremony  of  inftallation  included  the  solemn  putting-on 
of  the  trousers  and  drinking  from  the  “  cup  of  manhood  ”  {ka*s  ai- 
futuwa)^  which  contained  not  wine,  but  salt  and  water.  The  order 
took  over  from  its  SiSfi  progenitors  a  fiditious  genealogy  back  to  the 
Caliph  ‘All  (see  Chap.  II,  note  4),  and  continued  to  exid  for  some 
time  after  the  reign  of  Nisir  in  a  languishing  date.  The  Brotherhood 
which  Ibn  Battilta  found  in  Konia,  and  which  was  didinguished  from 
the  other  guilds  in  Anatolia  by  its  special  insignia  of  the  trousers  and 
its  claim  to  spiritual  descent  from  ‘All,  was  probably  a  relic  of  the  order 
founded  by  the  romantic  Caliph.  The  remaining  Anatolian  organiza¬ 
tions  seem  to  have  been  local  trade-guilds  with  a  very  drong  infusion 
of  Sufism,  oddly  combined  with  a  political  tendency  towards  local 
self-government  and  the  keeping  in  check  of  the  tyranny  of  the  Turkish 
sultans.  (See  generally  Thoming,  Turkische  Bib/iotbek,  Band  XVI 
(Berlin,  1913),  and  Wacyf  Boutros  Ghali,  La  Tradition  Chevaleresque 
des  Arabes  (Paris,  1919),  pp.  1-3  3)«

The  Khanate  of  Qipchaq  or  the  Golden  Horde  was  the  we^ern- 
mofr  of  the  four  great  Mongol  Khanates  e^blished  in  the  thirteenth

the  more  powerful,  and  their  territories  extended  from  Kiev  and  the 
Caucasus  to  the  Aral  Sea  and  Khiva.  Sultan  Muhammad  tjzbeg, 
who  reigned  from  1312  to  1340,  was  one  of  the  greater  of  the  Khans 
of  the  Blue  Horde.

tion  to  Muslim  ears  of  a  foreign  name,  Simari  being  a  word  familiar 
to  theologians  as  the  legendary  ance^or  of  the  Samaritans),  is  the 
Malayalam  word  Sdmutiri  or  Sdmuri  meaning  “  Sea-king,”  more 
familiar  to  European  readers  in  its  Portuguese  form  Zamorin. 

The  purpose  of  these  was  to  tow  the  junk  in  calm  weather,  as 
Ibn  Battuta  explains  below  (p.  278). 

Although  a  considerable  part  of  the  di^nce  between  Calicut 
and  Quilon  may  be  traversed  by  inland  waterways,  it  does  not  seem 
possible  to  go  the  whole  way  by  water.  Ibn  Battuta  here,  as  again  in 
the  description  of  his  travels  in  China,  neglefts  the  land  Plages. 

Quilon,  ranked  by  Ibn  Battuta  with  Calicut,  was  from  very  early 
times  the  transhipment  port  for  the  Chinese  trade.  It  is  mentioned 
by  the  Arab  and  Persian  sailors  of  the  ninth  century  under  the  name  of 
Kawlam-Malay,  and  fellv  into  decay,  like  its  rival  Calicut,  in  the 
sixteenth  century.  Yule  sugge^s  that  the  title  Tirawarl  given  by  Ibn 
Battuta  to  its  ruler  may  be  the  Tamil-Sanskrit  compound  Tiru-pati 
“  Holy  Lord  ”  {Cathay,  IV,  40). 

“  Always  a  sign  that  things  were  going  badly  with  Ibn  Battuta  ” 
(Yule). 

Sh^liyit,  the  Portuguese  Chiliate  or  Chale,  now  Bey  pore,  6^ 
miles  south  of  Calicut-  The  fabrics  manufadhtred  here  were  of  various 
kinds,  and  the  name  shall  is  ^ill  used  for  a  soft  cotton  fabric.  It  is 
possible  that  the  name  of  this  town  is  the  origin  of  the  French  chale, 
and  hence  our  shawl.

The  Arabic  and  Persian  writers  (like  Marco  Polo)  conventionally 
use  the  term  Qdn  or  Qa*dn  for  the  “  Great  Khan  ”  of  the  Mongols. 
It  is  not,  however,  as  Yule  considered,  a  difiPerent  title  from  the  ordinary 
Turkish  title  Khdqdn  (see  Shiratori,  Memoirs  of  Research  Deft,  of  the 
Toyo  Bunko^  No.  i,  Tokyo,  1926,  pp.  19-26). 

Sin-kakn  is  an  Arabicized  form  of  the  Persian  Chin-kaMn,  for 
Sanscrit  Mahicfna= Great  China,  which  is  also  the  meaning  of  the 
Arabic  name  Sin  as-Sin. 

The  text  is  defective  at  this  point,  due  either  to  the  miswriting 
of  a  word,  or  to  the  omission  of  another  word. 

Ibn  Battfita’s  route  up  the  river  ”  from  Ts’wan-chow  to  Canton 
is,  in  the  nature  of  things,  uncertain.  Yule  thinks  of  a  route  up  the 
Min  from  Foochow,  and  down  the  upper  reaches  of  the  Kan  to  the 
Pei-kiang  via  the  Mei-ling  Pass.  It  seems  a  peculiarly  roundabout 
journey,  when  much  more  direft  communication  is  offered  by  the 
Mei  and  the  Tung,  if  these  are  navigable. 

This  temple  has  not  been  identified  with  any  certainty.  Yule 
suggefrs  that  it  is  the  Temple  of  Glory  and  Filial  Duty^  near  the  N.W. 
comer  of  the  modem  city. 

The  site  of  the  Rampart  of  Gog  and  Magog,  the  building  of 
which  is  described  in  the  Koran  and  attributed  to  Alexander  the  Great, 
was  a  landing  problem  to  the  Arabic  geographers.  It  was  generally 
regarded  as  lying  at  the  north-eafrem  end  of  the  habitable  world,  and 
was  vaguely  confused  with  the  Great  Wall  of  China.  But  Ibn  Batttita 
could  ^ve  had  ho  idea  that  China  was  within  the  Wall,  and  his  quefrion 
appears  to  have  been  put  at  random,  perhaps  on  hearing  some  chance 
reference  to  the  Great  Wall. 

Marco  Polo  also  speaks  of  a  race  of  cannibals  in  the  mountains

between  Fukien  and  Kiang-si  or  Che-kiang  (II,  225  ;  H.  Schmidt- 
thenner  in  Zeitschrift  der  Ges.fur  Erdkunde  zu  Berlin^  1927,  p.  388). 

The  identification  of  Qan-jan-fi5  is  ^ill  uncertain.  If  Ibn 
Battfita  is  corre6I  in  placing  it  between  Zaytfin  and  Khansi,  its  position 
will  depend  on  the  route  which  he  followed.  Yule  identified  it  with 
Kien-chang-fu  on  the  Fu-ho  in  the  province  of  Kiang-si,  and  the  next 
Ration  Baywam  Qutlfi  with  the  Po-yang  Sea.  The  objedions  to  this 
identification  are  that  (i)  it  involves  a  very  roundabout  journey  to 
Hang-cho\y,  and  would  indeed  cut  out  Hang-chow  altogether ; 
(2)  there  is  no  evidence  for  the  exigence  of  a  frequented  trade- 
route  (such  as  Ibn  Battuta’s  route  is  represented  to  be)  through  Kien- 
chang-fu. 

Since  Ibn  Battfita  took  31  days  for  the  journey  to  Hang-chow, 
while  Marco  Polo  took  27,  travelling  in  the  reverse  diredion,  there  arc 
very  good  grounds  for  assuming  that  their  routes  were  subdantiaUy 
the  same.  In  this  case  the  mod  natural  identification  for  Qan-jan-fti 
is  Fuchow.  In  favour  of  this  are:  (i)  the  size  of  the  city,  with 
a  governor  of  its  own  and  a  large  garrison  (which  corresponds  very 
well  with  Marco  Polo’s  description)  ;  (2)  the  arrival  of  “  a  very  large 
vessel  ”  at  the  port,  since  Marco  Polo  expressly  dates  that  “  From 
Zayton  ships  come  this  way  right  up  to  the  city  of  Fuju  by  the  river 
I  have  told  you  of,  and  ’tis  in  this  way  that  the  precious  wares  of  India 
come  hither.”  The  name  which  Marco  Polo  gives  to  the  didrid  of 
Fuchow,  Chonka  or  Concha  (the  proper  name  of  the  city  being  Chin- 
kiang),  may  possibly  explain  the  transformation  into  Qan-jan-ffi. 
On  the  other  hand,  Marco  Polo  allows  only  five  days  from  Fuchow 
to  Zaytfin,  which  would  sugged  some  place  further  up  the  Min  river 
(on  the  navigability  of  the  Min  see  Marco  Poloy  II,  234). 

Dulaurier  suggeded  that  Qan-jan-fu  may  dand  for  Chin-kiang-fu 
at  the  jundion  of  the  Yang-tsi  and  the  Grand  Canal,  in  which  case 
it  should  come  between  Khansd  and  Khdn-bdliq  (Peking).  Similar 
indances  of  misplacing  are,  as  will  have  been  noticed,  not  infrequent 
with  Ibn  Battfita,  but  Chin-kiang-fu  scarcely  seems  large  enough  to 
fit  his  description.  M.  Ferrand  takes  Qan-jan-fii  to  be  Marco  Polo’s 
Kenjanfu,  the  old  capital  of  China,  now  Si-an-fu  on  the  Wei  river  in 
Shen-si,  which  was  called  Khumdin  by  the  Arabic  geographers.  This 
identification,  however,  hangs  together  with  M.  Ferrand’s  thesis  that 
Ibn  Battiita  did  not  go  to  China  at  all.  It  is  safed  to  assume  either 
that  Qan-jan-ffi  was  a  name  used  by  the  Muslim  merchants  for  Fuchow 
(like  Zaytun  for  Ts’wan-chow),  or  that  Ibn  Battiita  has  confused  two 
similar-sounding  names. 

The  Ghfita  is  the  name  given  to  the  wide  plain  covered  with 
fruit-trees  around  Damascus.

The  Limiyun  of  Ibn  Battdta  are  taken  bj^  Delafosse  and  Marquart 
to  be  the  inhabitants  of  the  Kebbe  (Kiba)  di^id.  There  is,  however,

from  “  Yuff  in  the  country  of  the  Limfs  ”  (see  next  note),  which  was 
didant  a  month’s  journey  from  there.  For  this  trans-continental 
trade  see  note  33  below.  The  word  Nyam-nyam  finally  became 
particularized  as  the  name  of  a  cannibal  tribe  in  Belgian  Congo. 
Meanwhile  it  had  passed  into  Mediterranean  folklore ;  F.  W.  Hasluck 
heard  from  an  Albanian  muleteer  of  “  an  entirely  new  kind  of  vampire 
called  Niam-Niam  soi,  which  he  has  seen.  You  know  it  because 
(i)  it  is  excessively  fond  of  liver  and  (2)  has  donkey’s  teeth  and  (3)  large 
feet”  (Cooley,  112  Hartmann  in  XV*^  172  ;  Hasluck, 

Letters  on  Religion  and  Folklore y  9). 

Oooley’s  identification  (p.  93)  of  Yuff  with  Nupe,  on  the  left 
bank  of  the  Niger  between  Jebba  and  Lokoja,  has  been  accepted  by 
all  later  authorities. 

In  thus  linking  the  Niger  on  to  the  Nile  (probably  by  way  of  the 
Bahr  al-Ghazdl)  Ibn  Battuta  is  at  lead  professing  the  less  erroneous 
of  two  wrong  views  commonly  held  before  the  discoveries  of  Mungo 
Park.  Idrlsl,  followed  by  Leo  Africanus  and  many  early  European 
geographers,  imagined  the  Niger  to  flow  wed,  and  identified  it  with 
the  Senegal  river. 

The  Chridian  kingdom  of  Nubia  was  invaded  by  the  sultans  of 
Egypt  on  several  occasions  between  1272  and  1323.  These  expedi¬ 
tions,  which  were  produdive  of  no  advantage  to  Egypt,  hadened  the 
break-up  of  the  Nubian  kingdom,  and  early  in  the  fourteenth  century 
Dongola  fell  into  the  hands  of  the  Arab  tribe  of  Kanz  or  Kanz  ad-Dawla, 
formerly  the  hereditary  amirs  of  Aswdn.  It  is  the  chief  of  this  tribe 
whom  Ibn  Battfita  calls  by  the  name  of  Ibn  Kanz  ad-Dln,  and  who, 
though  not  himself  a  convert,  may  be  reckoned  quite  fairly  as  the  fird 
Muslim  king  of  Nubia  (Marquart,  252-4).

The  following  is  a  brief  account  of  the  early  negro  empires. 

The  earlier  Sudanic  empire  was  that  of  Ghdna  (which  was  really 
the  title  of  its  later  Soninke  rulers).  This  empire  was  founded  about 
the  fourth  century,  apparently  by  some  white  immigrants.  The  site 
of  its  capital  seems  to  have  changed  more  than  once.  From  the  ninth 
to  the  eleventh  century  the  Soninke  of  Kumbi  were  makers  of  the 
Gh£na  empire,  until  its  destruction  by  the  Almoravids  of  Morocco  in 
1076.  A  number  of  small  states  were  con^ituted  on  its  ruins,  and 
one  of  these,  the  Soninke  dynasty  of  the  Kannte,  whose  capital  was  at 
Sosso  (to  the  weft  of  Sansanding),  recaptured  Ghina  in  1203  and 
reftored  the  Soninke  empire.  To  this  was  due  also  the  foundation 
of  WaMta,  as  the  Muslim  inhabitants  of  Ghdna,  refusing  to  live  under 
infidel  rule,  eftablished  themselves  at  the  water-point  of  Waldta  or 
Blru  (see  note  6).  The  conqueror,  Sumanguru,  was  killed  in  battle 
in  1235  the  Malinke,  whose  king  Sunjdta  or  Mdrf  Jita  annexed 
the  Soninke  empire,  was  converted  to  Islam  (see  p,  329),  and  eftablished 
the  new  capital  at  Mill!.  He  captured  and  deftroyed  Ghina  in  1 240, 
a.nd  died  in  1255.  After  a  succession  of  rulers,  the  next  emperor  of 
importance  was  Mfisi  (Ibn  Battuta’s  Mansd  Musa),  in  whose  reign 
(13^7-32)  the  M^Ui  empire  reached  its  wideft  dimensions.  Mfisd 
was  the  grandson  of  a  sifter  of  Sunjita.  The  reign  of  his  son  and 

380

successor  Mans^  Maghdn  (1332-6)  marks  a  brief  retrogression,  but 
under  Miisi’s  brother  Sulaymdn  (1336-59),  the  Mdllf  regained  much 
of  their  power  and  pre^ge.  With  his  death  there  set  in  a  sharp 
decline,  accentuated  by  civil  wars.  The  Mill!  kingdom,  however, 
^ill  remained  the  mo^  powerful  of  the  Niger  ^ates  until  the  rise  of 
the  Songhay  kingdom  (see  note  32),  and  did  not  finally  disappear 
until  1670.

Timbuktu  was  annexed  by  Mansd  Musd  after  the  conqueft  of

Gao  or  Gaogao  (which  is  apparently  a  variant  of  the  original 
name  Kfigha)  was  an  important  trading  ftation  at  the  convergence

in  the  eleventh  century  it  became  the  capital  of  the  ftate  of  Songhay 
(Songhoy),  on  the  conversion  to  Islam  of  the  firft  Songhay  dynafty, 
which  is  said  to  have  been  of  Berber  origin.  The  Songhay  kingdom 
was  annexed  to  Mill!  by  Mansd  Mfisd  in  1325,  but  in  1335  the

NOTES 

dyna^  was  re-e^ablished  (with  th^  title  of  sonnf)^  though  it  ^ill

The  Songhay  empire  was  broken  up  and  the  dyna^  extinguished  by

The  exigence  of  a  cowry  exchange  in  the  Milll  empire,  alongside 
the  salt  exchange,  is  conclusive  evidence  of  the  commercial  relations 
across  the  African  continent  referred  to  in  note  14,  as  cowries  are  found 
in  Africa  only  on  the  ea^  coa^  between  the  Equator  and  Mozambique 
(Grande  Encyclopidie  s.v.  Cauri).  In  Ibn  Battuta’s  time,  however, 
cowries  were  imported  by  merchants  from  the  north  (al-‘Omari  75-76). 

The  description  of  the  Bardima  tribe,  and  particularly  of  their 
women,  corresponds  very  closely  with  Barth’s  description  of  the 
Tagh^ma  tribe  to  the  south  and  S.W.  of  Air. 

Tagaddi  or  Takaddi  was  at  this  time  the  largefl  town  in  the 
Tuareg  country.  Its  Berber  sultan,  who  was  nominally  subjeft  to 
the  Emperor  of  MdlH,  is  probably  to  be  regarded  as  the  ruling  chief 
of  the  Massufa  (Sanhija).  The  problem  of  the  site  of  Tagaddi  is 
not  yet  cleared  up.  It  is  generally  taken,  on  the  basis  of  Barth’s 
identification,  to  be  Tegidda  n’Tisemt,  97  miles  W.N.W.  of  Agades. 
Barth  added  that  although  “  nothing  is  known  of  the  exi^lence  of  copper 
hereabouts,”  a  red  salt  is  obtained  from  mines  there.  Gautier  and 
Chudeau  (Missions  au  Sahara^  II,  257)  also  remark  on  the  absence  of 
copper  in  the  Sahara  except  at  Tamegroun  in  the  Ougarta  range 
(29.15  N.,  1.40  W.),  and  ^tate  that  all  the  copper  used  in  Air  and  in 
Ahaggar  comes  from  Europe.  The  absence  of  copper  at  Tegidda  is 
confirmed  by  F.  R.  Rodd,  who  thinks  that  Ibn  Battfita’s  Tagadd^ 
mu^  be  looked  for  “  at  some  considerable  di^nce  south  of  Agades  ” 
(People  of  the  Feil^  452-6).  The  meaning  of  the  word  Tegidda^ 
according  to  the  latter,  is  “  a  small  hollow  where  water  colle6b,”  and 
the  name  is  applied  to  a  number  of  different  places  (Cf  II,  193  ; 

Marquart,  9  8) .  The  exigence  of  copper  mines  at  T agaddd  is,  however, 
confirmed  by  al-‘Omarf  on  the  authority  of  Mansd  Musd  (tr.  Demom- 
bynes,  I,  80-81). 

Ktibar  is  Gobir,  the  country  north  of  the  present  Sokoto,  and 
consequently  bordering  on  Tagaddi  to  the  south.  Whether  Zdghiy 
^nds  here  for  the  diftrid  S.W.  of  Timbuktu,  or  for  the  central  areas 
round  Kanem  and  Wadai,  vaguely  known  as  Zaghiwa,  is  quite 
uncertain. 

Bamfi  here  Elands  for  Kanem,  rather  than  Bornu  in  Nigeria.  The 
empire  of  Kanem  at  this  period  extended  across  the  central  Sahara 
northwards  to  Fezzan  and  ea^wards  towards  D^r  Ffir,  as  well  as 
into  Northern  Nigeria,  This  Idris  (1353-76,  not  to  be  confused  with 

382 


NOTES 


the  famous  Mai  Idris  of  Bornu  in  the  sixteenth  century)  was  the  son 
of  one  Ibr^m  Nikdle,  who  claimed  to  be  of  South  Arabian  descent 
and  was  sultan  of  Kanem  from  1307-26.  The  concealment  of  the 
king  was  due  to  a  belief  in  the  magical  qualities  of  his  office  (Barth, 
I,  638-9  ;  Meek,  Northern  Nigeria^  I,  254). 

Jawjawa,  more  often  spelled  Kawkaw  or  Kukii,  the  Gaogao  of 
Leo  Africanus,  is  either  Kdka  on  Lake  Fitri  in  Wadai,  S.E.  of  Kanem 
or  else  Ktika  in  Bomu  (Marquart  95  jf.;  Hartmann  in 
XV^,  176  jf*.).  I  have  not  been  able  to  trace  the  Muwartabun  or 
Mdrtabtin. 

The  corps  of  wusfdn  or  “  guards  ”  at  the  court  of  the  sultan  of 
Morocco  formed  the  nucleus  of  a  landing  army,  as  di^inft  from  the 
tribal  militia  {Masdiik  al-Ahtdr,  tr.  Demombynes,  Index  j.e'.). 
Demombynes  reads,  with  one  MS.,  Initiyun  in  place  of  Yandtibiin 
{ihid,  210,  n.  2). 

Kdhir  is  a  variant  of  the  name  Air,  given  to  the  sparsely  populated 
hilly  country  lying  to  the  south  of  In  Azawa  or  Asiu,  the  well  referred 
to  below  at  the  point  where  the  routes  leading  to  Twit  and  Egypt 
divide.  .  It  is  ilrange  that  Ibn  Battuta  should  apparently  refer  to 
Kihir  as  separated  from  the  main  ridge  by  a  three  days’  march. 

Haggar  or  Hoggar,  the  Berber  (Tuareg)  tribes  inhabiting  the 
central  Saharan  massif,  the  ancient  Atlas  mountains,  now  called  Ahaggar 
after  its  inhabitants. 

Biida  lies  at  the  northern  end  of  the  Twit  valley  at  28  N.,  0.30  E 
An  account  of  the  di^rift  and  its  hi^ory  is  given  by  Gautier  and 
Chudeau,  Missions  an  Sahara  (Paris,  1908),  I,  250.  According  to 
the  Arabic  geographers,  locusts  were  also  eaten  by  the  inhabitants 
of  Marrikush.
